**Part 1**

- the part where Ms Chua asked you to
- the part where you were asked to *(shown above)*

</details>

[← 32](#c32) · [Next → 34](#c34) · [Contents](#contents)

### <a id="c34"></a>34. The Figure, Cited

[← 33](#c33) · [Next → 35](#c35) · [Contents](#contents)

A review of why your old scheme struggled has just cited a figure: 31%. That was its real usage rate, the one you put on a slide years ago instead of the range you'd been asked for. Someone senior asks, carefully, who presented it.

**A. Say it was you.**

> The questioner nods slowly and makes a note. A month later you're asked to help redesign the scheme: the kind of request that comes before a promotion.

**B. Say it was a team effort.**

> It wasn't, entirely. Your team knows that, and it does you more good with them than the other answer would have done upstairs.

<details><summary>This card varies (1 part)</summary>

Each time the card is shown, it is put together from one option of each part. The text above is one of them. Under an option, the words it brings to the rest of the card.

**Part 1**

- Ms Chua, who wanted the range back then,
  - Ms Chua
- someone senior *(shown above)*
  - The questioner

</details>

[← 33](#c33) · [Next → 35](#c35) · [Contents](#contents)

### <a id="c35"></a>35. 11:40pm, Level 9

[← 34](#c34) · [Next → 36](#c36) · [Contents](#contents)

It's 11:40pm, and your paper has come back for the fourth time with comments from the people who have to clear it. It's due upstairs in the morning. Faizal, a Management Executive who reports to you, is still at his desk, waiting to see what you decide. The dinner you promised to be home for was at eight.

**A. Finish it tonight.**

> It's cleared at 1:20am, with Faizal proofreading the annexes. You're both thanked in one email of one word. Dinner is in the fridge, with a note that's mostly punctuation.

**B. Go. Send it in the morning.**

> You send Faizal home first, and make it home yourself in time for dessert. The paper goes in at 8:40am, and the world doesn't end. You make a careful note that it didn't.

<details><summary>When it comes back (2 scenes)</summary>

The second time this comes round, it is written for the habit you have formed, which is the first choice. It is the last time you are asked.

---

*If your habit so far is “Finish it tonight.”:*

**Late, Again**

It's late again, a paper is due upstairs in the morning, and Faizal, who reports to you, is still at his desk, watching what you do. These nights always end the same way: you stay and finish, whatever was planned at home. What you do tonight is what you'll do every night after it.

**A. Finish it tonight, as usual.**

> Cleared at 1:10am, with Faizal on the annexes again. The thanks is one word, as before. At home the note on the fridge has stopped being punctuation and become a list.

**B. Go home. Send it in the morning, this once.**

> You send Faizal home first, then go yourself. The paper goes in at 8:40am and nobody upstairs can tell. At home, the dinner is what people remember.

---

*If your habit so far is “Go. Send it in the morning.”:*

**Late, Again**

It's late again, a paper is due upstairs in the morning, and Faizal, who reports to you, is waiting to see what you do. These nights always end the same way: you go home on time and send it in the morning, and the building hasn't fallen down yet. What you do tonight is what you'll do every night after it.

**A. Go home, as you always do.**

> You're home for dinner, and the paper goes in at 8:40am. Upstairs never asks when it was written. Two people on your floor now leave on time too, and say it was your idea.

**B. Stay tonight, and finish it.**

> You stay, and Faizal stays because you did. It's cleared at 1am. Upstairs says thank you, once, and the next morning Faizal looks at you as if you'd changed the rules.

</details>

<details><summary>Other versions (2)</summary>

The same choices, with the same consequences, in another scene. A career is shown one of them.

---

*Version 2: Sunday, 4pm*

You're at a friend's birthday party on a Sunday afternoon when an email from upstairs asks for a brief by eight tomorrow morning. Upstairs will judge you by whether it's there at eight, not by when you wrote it. Everyone else at the table is holding cake.

**A. Find a quiet corner and write it now.**

> You write it on your phone behind the cake table, and it's approved by nine. In the party photographs you're the back of a head, bent over a screen.

**B. Stay at the party. Write it at six tomorrow.**

> You sing, badly, and help cut the cake. The brief goes in at 7:55am. Upstairs never asks when it was written, and that teaches you something you try to remember.

---

*Version 3: 10:50pm, One Comment*

At 10:50pm your paper comes back from upstairs with one comment: "Can we tighten?" It's already two pages, it's due at nine tomorrow, and whoever wrote the comment is clearly still awake and waiting.

**A. Tighten it tonight.**

> Two pages become one and a half. The half you cut was the only part anyone would have disagreed with, so the paper sails through.

**B. Reply that it's already tight, and go to bed.**

> You reply at 10:52pm that it's already tight, and go to bed. In the morning there's a thumbs-up, which in this building counts as a concession.

</details>

[← 34](#c34) · [Next → 36](#c36) · [Contents](#contents)

### <a id="c36"></a>36. The Classification

[← 35](#c35) · [Next → 37](#c37) · [Contents](#contents)

HDB, the government's housing board, is planning a new estate, and you have to decide which of its projects are Standard, Plus or Prime. The label decides how long owners must live there and what they repay when they sell. You have a map, a set of criteria, and three neighbouring blocks that are, in every way a resident would notice, identical.

**A. Apply the criteria exactly, and let the line fall where it falls.**

> The line falls between two of the three blocks. Years from now, two neighbours on the same corridor will have different rules for selling the same kind of flat, and both will know exactly why.

**B. Move the line so it follows the road.**

> The road makes a tidy boundary, and the criteria get a footnote. Nobody reads footnotes, until somebody does.

**C. Write a note on the three blocks, and send it up.**

> Your note asks a question nobody wanted asked. It's answered eventually, in a way you can live with, by someone more senior who takes the credit for the question.

[← 35](#c35) · [Next → 37](#c37) · [Contents](#contents)

### <a id="c37"></a>37. Ninety-Nine Years

[← 36](#c36) · [Next → 38](#c38) · [Contents](#contents)

A residents' committee from a 1970s estate built by HDB, the government's housing board, has written in. Their leases are ninety-nine years long and about half used up. The letter is polite, well researched, and asks one question: what happens to their flats when the lease runs out. The answer is in the policy. They've read the policy.

**A. Meet them, and explain it in person.**

> Two hours at a void deck, the shaded open space under one of their blocks. You explain the policy, and then you listen to what it means to them. That's a different conversation, and a longer one.

**B. Reply in writing, accurately and kindly.**

> Your letter is accurate and kind. It's pinned on the noticeboard beside the lift, where everyone reads it and most people believe it.

**C. Invite them to the consultation on redevelopment.**

> They come, all eleven of them, with a folder. Their folder is better organised than yours.

[← 36](#c36) · [Next → 38](#c38) · [Contents](#contents)

### <a id="c38"></a>38. The Banding

[← 37](#c37) · [Next → 39](#c39) · [Contents](#contents)

Under Full Subject-Based Banding, students in the same class take different subjects at different levels, so a school's timetable no longer fits inside its classes. A principal has written to the ministry to say the school's teachers are now, in effect, air traffic controllers. The ministry's reply is yours to decide.

**A. Fund the timetabling software the principal asked for.**

> The software mostly works. The teachers are now air traffic controllers with radar, which they say is an improvement.

**B. Send a guidebook and a hotline number.**

> The guidebook is thorough and the hotline is staffed. The principal's reply thanks you for both, in a tone you'll remember.

**C. Spend a week in the school, and find out.**

> You spend a week with a timetable on a wall the size of a car park, and come back with three changes that fit on one page. They work.

[← 37](#c37) · [Next → 39](#c39) · [Contents](#contents)

### <a id="c39"></a>39. The Merger List

[← 38](#c38) · [Next → 40](#c40) · [Contents](#contents)

Fewer children are born every year, so some schools must merge, and you're drawing up the list. Every school on it will lose its name or its building. Every school on it also has an alumni association, a school song, and a former principal who is still alive and writes letters.

**A. Draw the list on the numbers.**

> The list is defensible, line by line. The letters arrive line by line too, many of them beautifully written, and all of them signed.

**B. Keep the oldest names, whatever the numbers say.**

> The old names survive. Two newer schools with better numbers don't, and their students will remember the year their school was described as "consolidated".

**C. Propose that merged schools keep both names.**

> The names are hyphenated. The school songs aren't, and the first combined assembly is, by every account, loud.

[← 38](#c38) · [Next → 40](#c40) · [Contents](#contents)

### <a id="c40"></a>40. The Bed Crunch

[← 39](#c39) · [Next → 41](#c41) · [Contents](#contents)

Public hospitals are over ninety per cent full, and emergency waiting times are in the papers again. You've been asked for a paper on transitional care: beds outside the hospitals for patients who are well enough to leave but have nowhere ready to go. The Minister wants something to announce.

**A. Recommend more transitional beds, and say where the nurses will come from.**

> Your paper has a section on nurses that nobody asked for and everybody quotes. The beds are approved. Staffing them is, as you said it would be, the hard part.

**B. Recommend the beds, and leave the nurses to a later paper.**

> The beds are approved quickly. A later paper, not yours, discovers there aren't enough nurses, and cites yours as "an important first step".

**C. Recommend better discharge planning instead.**

> It's the right answer and the less announceable one. The hospitals like it. The press office asks whether there's a version with a number in it.

[← 39](#c39) · [Next → 41](#c41) · [Contents](#contents)

### <a id="c41"></a>41. The Premiums

[← 40](#c40) · [Next → 42](#c42) · [Contents](#contents)

Premiums for MediShield Life, the national health insurance everyone pays into, are going up, and the actuaries have done the sums: the increase is what it is. Your job is to decide how it's put to the public: all at once or over several years, and with how much subsidy shown beside it. Whatever you choose, someone will add it up.

**A. Present the full number, with the subsidies next to it.**

> The increase is large, the subsidies are larger for the people who need them, and the slide says both. It's the least comfortable slide in the deck, and the only one nobody later has to correct.

**B. Phase it over three years, so no single year looks large.**

> Each year looks reasonable. Within a week somebody adds the three years together, in a letter to the newspaper, with a table in it.

**C. Ask the actuaries to walk you through their assumptions first.**

> Two days in a room with the actuaries. You come out understanding medical cost inflation better than anyone in your division, and slightly worried about it.

[← 40](#c40) · [Next → 42](#c42) · [Contents](#contents)

### <a id="c42"></a>42. The Dormitory Standards

[← 41](#c41) · [Next → 43](#c43) · [Contents](#contents)

Your division has drafted new standards for migrant worker dormitories: fewer beds to a room, more toilets, better ventilation, more space per worker. The dormitory operators have written to say they'll pass the cost on to employers. The employers have written to say they won't absorb it. You decide what holds.

**A. Hold the standards, and phase the deadline.**

> The standards hold. The deadline moves twice, in writing, with reasons. Two years later the rooms are measurably less crowded. It isn't a headline, and that's the point.

**B. Hold the standards and the deadline.**

> Three operators complain about you, by name, to three different people. The rooms are less crowded on schedule.

**C. Soften the space requirement for existing dormitories.**

> Everyone is relieved, including you, briefly. New dormitories meet the new standard. The old ones keep to the old one, for longer than anyone said they would.

[← 41](#c41) · [Next → 43](#c43) · [Contents](#contents)

### <a id="c43"></a>43. The Wage Ladder

[← 42](#c42) · [Next → 44](#c44) · [Contents](#contents)

Your division is extending the Progressive Wage Model, the ladder of minimum wages tied to skills, to another sector. The union wants the ladder steeper, so pay rises faster. The employers want it longer, so pay rises slowly. The workers in the sector, when you finally meet some, want to know when.

**A. Back the steeper ladder.**

> The ladder ends up steeper than the employers wanted and gentler than the union asked for. In tripartite talks, where government, unions and employers settle wages together, that's a win for everyone and a story for nobody.

**B. Broker the longer, gentler ladder.**

> Everyone signs. The first rung is lower than you'd hoped. You tell yourself it's a ladder, and ladders get climbed.

**C. Take both versions back to the workers, and ask.**

> They choose the steeper one and ask for it sooner. You write that down exactly, and it goes into the paper sent upstairs. Workers' own words rarely get that far.

[← 42](#c42) · [Next → 44](#c44) · [Contents](#contents)

### <a id="c44"></a>44. Meet-the-People Session

[← 43](#c43) · [Next → 45](#c45) · [Contents](#contents)

An old school friend volunteers at her MP's Meet-the-People Session: every Tuesday night, residents bring their problems to their Member of Parliament, and volunteers write their appeal letters to agencies. She asks you to help. Most of the cases were decided correctly the first time. The sessions are run by the party's branch in the ward, and civil servants are meant to stay out of politics. She says lots of people help in their own time.

**A. Go every Tuesday for a year, in your own time.**

> Fifty-one Tuesdays, in your own T-shirt, never the branch one. You learn more about how policy lands than in all your years of writing it. One appeal you argue properly, for a woman everyone said was hopeless, wins. The MP notices.

↳ Can lead to: [45. The Block Party](#c45)

**B. Go a few times, enough to be seen.**

> You go four times, and make sure the MP sees you on two of them. You learn very little about how policy lands. The branch learns that you are interested.

**C. Decline. You are a civil servant, and mean to stay neutral.**

> Your friend says she completely understands. The branch secretary, standing next to her, also understands, and will remember your name if it ever comes up.

[← 43](#c43) · [Next → 45](#c45) · [Contents](#contents)

### <a id="c45"></a>45. The Block Party

[← 44](#c44) · [Next → 46](#c46) · [Contents](#contents)

Years ago, before politics, you spent a year of Tuesday nights writing appeal letters at a Meet-the-People Session. One letter, for a woman everyone said was hopeless, won. Now, at a block party in your ward, that woman is introducing you to forty of her neighbours. She has told them the story, in detail, more than once.

**A. Let her tell it again.**

> She tells it with improvements. By the end of the evening the appeal has overturned a decision, saved a business and possibly a marriage. Forty people will vote for the version with the marriage.

**B. Tell them the appeal worked because her case was good.**

> This is true. She tells them you are being modest, and now the modesty is part of the story too. It goes round the block faster than you can walk it.

[← 44](#c44) · [Next → 46](#c46) · [Contents](#contents)

### <a id="c46"></a>46. The New Reporting Officer

[← 45](#c45) · [Next → 47](#c47) · [Contents](#contents)

Ms Chua, your reporting officer until now, has been given more to run, so you now report to someone new from another ministry, who knows nothing about you and has brought two trusted people along. The newcomer will write your appraisals. Meanwhile the division's hardest file, a review the Permanent Secretary wants by December, has nobody on it.

**A. Make yourself indispensable on the hardest file.**

> The review takes eight months, and goes in before December. Your new reporting officer rates people strictly on what they've done, which turns out to be rarer than you'd thought, and suits you well.

**B. Be useful to the newcomer's two people.**

> You become the person they ask where things are and whom to see. It's a real position and a small one, and you hold it for as long as they stay.

**C. Keep your head down until the newcomer leaves.**

> The newcomer is in no hurry to leave. Meanwhile your appraisals go flat, and every promotion board for the rest of your career will see the line.

<details><summary>When it comes back (3 scenes)</summary>

The second time this comes round, it is written for the habit you have formed, which is the first choice. It is the last time you are asked.

---

*If your habit so far is “Make yourself indispensable on the hardest file.”:*

**The New Reporting Officer**

Ms Chua, your reporting officer until now, has been given more to run, and someone new from another ministry will write your appraisals. Since your first months you've gone for the hard work first, and the division's hardest file has nobody on it. What the newcomer sees this year is what your file will say about you for years.

**A. Take the hardest file, as you always have.**

> The review goes in before December. Your new reporting officer rates people on what they've done, and you've done a great deal. It suits you, and it's become what people expect of you.

**B. This time, start with the newcomer's two people.**

> You learn their names before you learn the newcomer's habits. By March they ask you where things are and whom to see. The hardest file goes to someone else, who is tired by December.

**C. Let someone else take the file. Keep your head down.**

> Someone else takes the hard file, and you do your own work, quietly. The newcomer forms a view of you anyway, and the view is "steady", which every promotion board after will read.

---

*If your habit so far is “Be useful to the newcomer's two people.”:*

**The New Reporting Officer**

Ms Chua, your reporting officer until now, has been given more to run, and someone new from another ministry will write your appraisals, with two trusted people in tow. Since your first months you've made a point of knowing who matters. What the newcomer sees this year is what your file will say about you for years.

**A. Make yourself useful to the newcomer's two people.**

> By Christmas you're the fourth chair at their lunch table. It's a real position and a small one, and you hold it for as long as they stay.

**B. Leave the networking. Take the hardest file.**

> You take the file nobody wanted, and finish it in eight months. Fewer people know your face this year. Your new reporting officer knows your work, which turns out to be the one that counts.

**C. Step back, and keep your head down.**

> You stop going to the lunches and do your own work. The newcomer barely knows your name by June, and the two trusted people have found someone else to ask.

---

*If your habit so far is “Keep your head down until the newcomer leaves.”:*

**The New Reporting Officer**

Ms Chua, your reporting officer until now, has been given more to run, and someone new from another ministry will write your appraisals. Since your first months you've kept your head down and done what's in front of you, well and without fuss. What the newcomer sees this year is what your file will say about you for years.

**A. Keep your head down, as you always have.**

> You do your own work well, and the newcomer notices it the way you notice a clock that keeps time. Your appraisals go flat, and every promotion board after will read the same line.

**B. This time, ask for the hardest file.**

> You ask for the file nobody wanted, and the newcomer, who doesn't know you're usually quiet, hands it over without surprise. It goes in before December. You're slightly surprised you asked.

**C. This time, get to know the newcomer's people.**

> You start having lunch with the newcomer's two people. They're friendly, and by March they're asking you where things are. You find you're good at this.

</details>

<details><summary>Other versions (2)</summary>

The same choices, with the same consequences, in another scene. A career is shown one of them.

---

*Version 2: Standing Up*

Your new reporting officer starts on Monday, and Ms Chua, who had the job until now, has moved up a floor to run something bigger. The newcomer spent twenty-two years in the army, holds the morning meeting standing up, and has brought two officers from the old unit. Nobody has briefed the newcomer on the year's most urgent file: the legal opinion that a decision due in March rests on.

**A. Brief the newcomer on the legal opinion yourself.**

> You brief your new reporting officer on the legal opinion in nine minutes, standing, and the March decision goes through. From then on you're asked for by surname, which in the newcomer's vocabulary is a commendation.

**B. Make yourself useful to the newcomer's two officers.**

> They know the newcomer; you know the building. By Christmas you're the fourth chair at their lunch table, and you keep it. It's the chair by the window.

**C. Do as you're told, and wait for the newcomer to move on.**

> The newcomer shows no sign of moving on. Your appraisals start to read like a roll call: present, correct, in line, and visible to every promotion board after.

---

*Version 3: The Deck*

Ms Chua, your reporting officer until now, is too busy to supervise you, so you're getting a new one: a mid-career hire from a consulting firm who wants everything as a deck, and has brought two former colleagues in on contract. This year's biggest decision rests on a file that won't fit on a slide: an Act, two amendments and a court case.

**A. Take the file that won't fit on a slide.**

> You walk your new reporting officer through the Act, the amendments and the court case in an afternoon. The newcomer is quick, and from then on rates you on what you know.

**B. Make yourself useful to the two consultants.**

> They need someone who knows how a paper really moves through the building, and you do. They bill for it. Their final report thanks you in a footnote, as "a key stakeholder".

**C. Wait for the new boss to go back to the private sector.**

> Your new boss likes it here, and stays. Your appraisals stop changing by so much as a word, and every promotion board after reads the identical years as one.

</details>

[← 45](#c45) · [Next → 47](#c47) · [Contents](#contents)

### <a id="c47"></a>47. The Ask

[← 46](#c46) · [Next → 48](#c48) · [Contents](#contents)

Xin Yi, who joined the service with you, stops you by the lift and asks you for a favour: a reference for a two-year posting to the Budget Division. Saying yes takes time. Saying no will be remembered. Your own paper is due the same week, and isn't finished.

**A. Say yes, and do it properly.**

> You write three paragraphs, all of them true, and send them before midnight. Xin Yi says you're owed one, and writes it in a notebook you've seen before.

**B. Say yes, and make sure it's seen.**

> You write three good paragraphs, and copy in two people who'll remember that you wrote them. Xin Yi says you're owed one, and writes it in a notebook you've seen before.

**C. Say no, and say why.**

> You explain that a reference from you would read as a favour, because it would be one. Xin Yi takes it well, and has asked someone else by lunchtime.

<details><summary>This card varies (3 parts)</summary>

Each time the card is shown, it is put together from one option of each part. The text above is one of them. Under an option, the words it brings to the rest of the card.

**Part 1**

- Xin Yi, who joined the service with you, *(shown above)*
  - Xin Yi
  - her
  - Xin Yi says you're owed one, and writes it in a notebook you've seen before.
  - Xin Yi takes it well, and has asked someone else by lunchtime.
- Xin Yi, who got the director's post in the reorganisation instead of you,
  - Xin Yi
  - her
  - Xin Yi says that makes the two of you even, and you both know it doesn't.
  - Xin Yi says that's fair, and reminds you, lightly, which of you got the post.
- Faizal, who reports to you,
  - Faizal
  - his
  - Faizal thanks you twice, the second time when nobody else is around to hear it.
  - Faizal nods, and doesn't ask you for anything else for some months.
- Faizal, who once proofread your annexes until 1:20am,
  - Faizal
  - his
  - Faizal thanks you once, and you understand it's for the annexes as well.
  - Faizal says that's fine, in very nearly the tone you used at 1:20am.
- Faizal, whom you once sent home before a deadline,
  - Faizal
  - his
  - Faizal thanks you, and leaves on time that evening, which you also take as thanks.
  - Faizal says that's fine, and leaves on time, which you take as the reply.
- Ms Chua, your reporting officer,
  - Ms Chua
  - her
  - Ms Chua remembers it in the meeting that decides your grade, the only room where it counts.
  - Ms Chua says that's perfectly fine, in the way the word is used upstairs.

**Part 2**

- a reference for a two-year posting to the Budget Division *(shown above)*
  - You write three paragraphs, all of them true, and send them before midnight.
  - You write three good paragraphs, and copy in two people who'll remember that you wrote them.
  - You explain that a reference from you would read as a favour, because it would be one.
- a second read of a paper that goes up at nine tomorrow
  - You read it twice and send back three comments, all of them fair, at 11:40pm.
  - You send back three comments, and mention at the morning meeting that you looked it over.
  - You say you can't do it justice tonight. It's true, and no help to the paper.
- someone to take her place at a Saturday dialogue session in Jurong
  - You spend the Saturday in Jurong with forty residents and a microphone that works half the time.
  - You take the Saturday session. Its photograph goes in the division newsletter, with you in front.
  - You keep your Saturday. The session goes ahead with nobody senior there, and is written up as "intimate".

**Part 3**

- Your own paper is due the same week, and isn't finished. *(shown above)*
- HR sent round a reminder about conflicts of interest this morning, in bold.
- Someone else on your floor asked you for the same thing an hour ago.
- Nobody else has asked you for anything all week.

</details>

[← 46](#c46) · [Next → 48](#c48) · [Contents](#contents)

### <a id="c48"></a>48. The Seventh Month

[← 47](#c47) · [Next → 49](#c49) · [Contents](#contents)

During the seventh month of the lunar calendar, when the spirits of the dead are said to roam, your division's online feedback form receives a compliment from a resident who passed away in 1974. It's courteous and specific. The service standard says every member of the public gets a reply within three working days. You must also rule whether his rating counts in the satisfaction survey, where your division's score is one decimal place short of its target.

**A. Rule that he counts. He is a member of the public.**

> He is counted. He rates your reply five out of five, and adds that it came faster than his last one, in 1973. His rating lifts the division to its target, and the result is presented at the next townhall.

**B. Rule that he does not. The survey covers living residents.**

> The reply still goes out within three working days, because the service standard makes no exception. The survey's definitions are amended to add the word "living", which had never occurred to anyone.

**C. Refer the question to a working group.**

> The working group meets in the eighth month, by which time he has stopped writing in, and recommends reviewing the question in the seventh month next year. It is reviewed then, and every year after.

[← 47](#c47) · [Next → 49](#c49) · [Contents](#contents)

### <a id="c49"></a>49. Standard Operating Procedure

[← 48](#c48) · [Next → 50](#c50) · [Contents](#contents)

Faizal, who reports to you, has just joined your section, and has been handed the official standard operating procedures: forty-one pages, last updated two reorganisations ago, and mostly out of date. You keep your own version on one page of a notebook. It's the reason you get things done faster than anyone else on the floor.

**A. Give Faizal your page.**

> Your page says which director reads only the first paragraph, which printer jams, and who in HR picks up before nine. Faizal is useful within a week, and photocopies it.

**B. Let Faizal learn it the way you did.**

> Faizal learns it by getting it wrong in front of the right people. It takes a year. Neither of you mentions it, and Faizal starts a notebook.

**C. Rewrite the forty-one pages around your one.**

> It takes three months of evenings. The division adopts your new procedures, with your name in the version history and nowhere else, and Faizal learns them in a weekend.

[← 48](#c48) · [Next → 50](#c50) · [Contents](#contents)

## <a id="stage-service"></a>The service

Assistant Director to Senior Director. Cards 50 to 120.

### <a id="c50"></a>50. Lo Hei

[← 49](#c49) · [Next → 51](#c51) · [Contents](#contents)

It's the division's Chinese New Year lunch, and the Permanent Secretary is at your table. After eleven minutes of lo hei, tossing the yusheng salad as high as chopsticks allow and shouting wishes for abundance, the Permanent Secretary turns to you and asks, properly: "So, Wei Ling, if your division had to stop one thing this year, what should it be?" The first project that comes to mind is your own.

**A. Your own project. Say why.**

> You have about ninety seconds, and you spend all of them on why your own work should stop. The Permanent Secretary stops eating to listen, and at the end says, "Say that again on Thursday, to the committee." The people who decide promotions will be in that room.

**B. Another division's project. Say why.**

> It's a reasonable answer, well argued, and the Permanent Secretary nods. The other division's project is reviewed in March. Its director never finds out who suggested it, and you never ask whether yours would have been next.

**C. Say that everything the division does matters.**

> "Mm," says the Permanent Secretary, and turns to the person on the other side. That's the end of it, and you both know it.

[← 49](#c49) · [Next → 51](#c51) · [Contents](#contents)

### <a id="c51"></a>51. The Report

[← 50](#c50) · [Next → 52](#c52) · [Contents](#contents)

Your ministry paid a consultancy \$1.9 million to work out why one of its schemes isn't working. Today they present their final deck, and it's your own idea: the one you set out in a minute paper, a memo to the people above you asking for a decision, which nobody acted on. Only your paragraph four, on how it could go wrong, is missing. The Deputy Secretary who hired them loves it.

**A. Say nothing. Let them have it.**

> The recommendation is adopted with enthusiasm. At the townhall it's described as "a fresh external perspective", and you clap with everyone else.

**B. Circulate your old minute paper.**

> You send the minute paper, paragraph four and all, to the fourteen people at the presentation. Nobody replies except a Deputy Secretary you've never met, who writes only to you: "Good paper." The one who hired the consultants reads it too.

↳ Can lead to: [52. Paragraph Four](#c52)

**C. Ask the consultants, in the Q&A, what their data source was.**

> The consultancy's partner checks the appendix and reads out the source: a minute paper from your division, written before the consultants were hired. Yours. The room enjoys this more than the partner does.

[← 50](#c50) · [Next → 52](#c52) · [Contents](#contents)

### <a id="c52"></a>52. Paragraph Four

[← 51](#c51) · [Next → 53](#c53) · [Contents](#contents)

When a consultancy's \$1.9 million deck repeated the advice in your old minute paper, you circulated the paper to everyone at the presentation. The ministry went with the consultants' version anyway, and it has now gone wrong exactly as your paragraph four, on the risks, said it would. Someone upstairs has found paragraph four.

**A. Let paragraph four speak for itself.**

> It's read aloud at a meeting you aren't at. Afterwards three people forward it to you, each believing they're the first.

**B. Offer to fix it.**

> You're allowed to. It takes a year, and then a consultancy is hired to evaluate your fix.

[← 51](#c51) · [Next → 53](#c53) · [Contents](#contents)

### <a id="c53"></a>53. The Forum Page

[← 52](#c52) · [Next → 54](#c54) · [Contents](#contents)

A member of the public has written to the Forum page, the letters page of the Straits Times, about a scheme your division runs. His letter is 340 words long, very angry, and right about every fact. Drafting the ministry's reply is your job, due to Ms Chua, your reporting officer, by Friday, and the paper will print whatever goes out.

**A. Draft the standard reply: thank him for his feedback.**

> Your reply says the scheme "is regularly reviewed", in 310 words that commit to nothing. It's approved in a single round, which tells you exactly what gets approved easily here.

**B. Draft a reply that admits he's right, and says what will change.**

> It goes through nine rounds of approval and comes out shorter, but the admission survives. He writes in again, to thank you. That letter doesn't get published.

**C. Look him up in the ministry's records first.**

> His record gives you his address in Ang Mo Kio and the school he taught at for thirty years. You close it without doing anything with it. But you did open it, and the system logs who opens what.

↳ Can lead to: [54. The Citizens' Panel](#c54) · [Everything Surfaced](#end-scandal)

[← 52](#c52) · [Next → 54](#c54) · [Contents](#contents)

### <a id="c54"></a>54. The Citizens' Panel

[← 53](#c53) · [Next → 55](#c55) · [Contents](#contents)

Years ago a retired schoolteacher wrote an angry, accurate letter to the Forum page about your scheme, and you looked up his address in the ministry's records. Now twelve members of the public, picked at random, are reviewing the scheme over three Saturdays. He's one of them, and he has brought the cutting.

**A. Tell him, privately, that you once looked up his address.**

> He considers this for a long time, then says that in thirty years of teaching he looked up many pupils' addresses, usually because he was worried about them, and he hopes you were at least that worried. The panel's report is fair. He wrote most of it.

**B. Thank him for his letter, in front of the panel.**

> He accepts the thanks with the face of a teacher accepting homework that has clearly been copied. The panel's report is fair, and has a section on responsiveness.

**C. Leave the sessions to Faizal.**

> Faizal reports back that the panel was constructive, and that one member asked, twice, whether you'd be coming.

[← 53](#c53) · [Next → 55](#c55) · [Contents](#contents)

### <a id="c55"></a>55. The Recruiter

[← 54](#c54) · [Next → 56](#c56) · [Contents](#contents)

A recruiter has found you on LinkedIn, which you'd forgotten you were on. A bank wants someone who understands how ministries think, and the salary is a number you read twice. Taking it means resigning from the service, and from whatever it had planned for you.

**A. Take it.**

> Your pass stops working on a Friday. On Monday you have a new one, and a view.

↳ Can lead to: [The Consultancy](#end-consultancy)

**B. Mention the offer, lightly, to HR.**

> The service doesn't match a bank's offer: your pay stays what your grade pays. HR quietly raises its private estimate of how far you'll go. Your bosses hear you were shopping around, and it shows in this year's appraisal.

**C. Decline. You're not finished here.**

> You delete the message. Then you undelete it, read the number again, and delete it.

[← 54](#c54) · [Next → 56](#c56) · [Contents](#contents)

### <a id="c56"></a>56. The Nomination

[← 55](#c55) · [Next → 57](#c57) · [Contents](#contents)

Your division can put one name forward this year for the Public Service Leadership Programme, the course that marks people out for the top jobs. Ms Chua, your reporting officer, wants your nomination by Friday, and it can be your own name. Everyone expects you to put forward the division's scholar, one of the high-flyers the government paid to study overseas, who is going places anyway.

**A. Nominate yourself.**

> It isn't done, putting your own name forward. It also isn't forbidden. Ms Chua writes your name down slowly, and a committee you'll never meet raises its estimate of how far you'll go.

**B. Nominate the obvious scholar.**

> The scholar was going anyway, and thanks you, warmly and sincerely, which somehow makes it worse. The officer who does the division's real work reads the shortlist on the intranet, like everyone else.

**C. Nominate the quiet officer who does all the actual work.**

> The quiet officer is astonished, and goes on the programme. For nine months the work that officer used to do lands on your desk. Your nominee will rise faster than you, and won't forget who made the nomination.

↳ Can lead to: [57. The Officer You Nominated](#c57) (or, at another grade, [#58](#c58), [#59](#c59), [#60](#c60), [#61](#c61))

[← 55](#c55) · [Next → 57](#c57) · [Contents](#contents)

### <a id="c57"></a>57. The Officer You Nominated

[← 56](#c56) · [Next → 58](#c58) · [Contents](#contents)

Over a decade ago, you put forward the quiet officer who did your division's real work for the leadership programme, instead of yourself. That officer is a Deputy Secretary now, and has been asked to suggest names for another Deputy Secretary post: the last rung below Permanent Secretary.

**A. Say nothing, and let your old nominee decide.**

> Your name goes in. Neither of you mentions the nomination from years ago. It was never a transaction, and it stays that way by not being mentioned.

**B. Say first that your old nominee owes you nothing.**

> Your old nominee says that is understood, and puts your name in anyway. That's how you learn the difference between a debt and a judgement.

[← 56](#c56) · [Next → 58](#c58) · [Contents](#contents)

### <a id="c58"></a>58. The Officer You Nominated

[← 57](#c57) · [Next → 59](#c59) · [Contents](#contents)

Over a decade ago, you nominated the quiet officer who did your division's real work for the leadership programme, instead of yourself. That officer has since overtaken you, is now a Deputy Secretary with a post to fill, and asks for you by name.

**A. Take it.**

> Neither of you mentions the nomination. You're given the hardest file in the division, and everything you write is read twice.

**B. Say first that your old nominee owes you nothing.**

> Your old nominee says that is understood, and asks for you anyway. That's how you learn the difference between a debt and a judgement.

[← 57](#c57) · [Next → 59](#c59) · [Contents](#contents)

### <a id="c59"></a>59. The Officer You Nominated

[← 58](#c58) · [Next → 60](#c60) · [Contents](#contents)

Over a decade ago, you nominated the quiet officer who did your division's real work for the leadership programme, instead of yourself. That officer is a Permanent Secretary now, younger than you and faster, and has been asked for names for another Permanent Secretary post, the last rung there is. You're a Deputy Secretary.

**A. Say nothing, and let your old nominee decide.**

> Your name goes in. Neither of you mentions the nomination from years ago, because the whole point of it was that it was never a transaction.

**B. Say first that your old nominee owes you nothing.**

> Your old nominee says that is understood, and puts your name in anyway. That's how you learn the difference between a debt and a judgement.

[← 58](#c58) · [Next → 60](#c60) · [Contents](#contents)

### <a id="c60"></a>60. The Officer You Nominated

[← 59](#c59) · [Next → 61](#c61) · [Contents](#contents)

Over a decade ago, you nominated the quiet officer who did your division's real work for the leadership programme, instead of yourself. That officer is a Permanent Secretary now too, and you sit on the same committees. Your old nominee has been asked, quietly, which fellow Permanent Secretaries the party should one day invite to tea: its first quiet approach to someone it might ask to stand for Parliament.

**A. Say nothing, and let your old nominee answer.**

> One name is given. You only find out whose from the way certain people start greeting you in lifts.

**B. Say first that your old nominee owes you nothing.**

> Your old nominee says that is understood, and gives your name anyway. That's how you learn the difference between a debt and a judgement.

[← 59](#c59) · [Next → 61](#c61) · [Contents](#contents)

### <a id="c61"></a>61. The Officer You Nominated

[← 60](#c60) · [Next → 62](#c62) · [Contents](#contents)

Over a decade ago, you put forward the quiet officer who did your old division's real work for the leadership programme, instead of yourself. That officer is a Permanent Secretary now. From time to time the Prime Minister's office asks Permanent Secretaries for a frank view of the politicians they work with, before deciding who goes up. Your old nominee has been asked about you.

**A. Say nothing, and let your old nominee answer.**

> The view goes in, and you never see it. At the next reshuffle the commentary calls you someone the civil service rates. That is the one review a politician never gets to read, and it helps.

**B. Say first that your old nominee owes you nothing.**

> Your old nominee says that is understood, and gives an honest view anyway, which is how you finally learn the difference between a debt and a judgement.

[← 60](#c60) · [Next → 62](#c62) · [Contents](#contents)

### <a id="c62"></a>62. Someone From Before

[← 61](#c61) · [Next → 63](#c63) · [Contents](#contents)

At a meeting of several agencies, you recognise the person chairing it. Years ago, before your degree, the two of you worked side by side at a public service counter, on the same grade. Your old colleague is three grades above you now, and chairs the committees that decide who gets noticed.

**A. Speak to your old colleague afterwards.**

> You're remembered at once, and without condescension, which you hadn't dared expect. Within the month you're on two committees that matter, and have been told plainly which one to care about.

**B. Say nothing. Your old colleague has moved on.**

> Your old colleague hadn't moved on, and simply hadn't seen you at the far end of the table. You think about the counter for a week, and then you stop.

[← 61](#c61) · [Next → 63](#c63) · [Contents](#contents)

### <a id="c63"></a>63. The Board Takes a Second Look

[← 62](#c62) · [Next → 64](#c64) · [Contents](#contents)

Someone on a promotion board has read several of your papers, and thinks your work is better than your grade suggests. Unusually, the board has been asked to look again at your file, and at HR's private estimate of the highest grade you'll ever reach, which is never shown to you.

**A. Make the case yourself, in writing.**

> Four pages. You don't argue that you've been treated unfairly, which would be true and useless. You argue what you'd do with the next grade. The estimate is revised, and nobody tells you by how much.

**B. Let the record speak.**

> The record does speak, in the dry language of appraisal forms, and it moves the board slightly less than four pages would have.

[← 62](#c62) · [Next → 64](#c64) · [Contents](#contents)

### <a id="c64"></a>64. The Inter-Ministry Committee

[← 63](#c63) · [Next → 65](#c65) · [Contents](#contents)

You're the secretariat for an Inter-Ministry Committee: you write its papers and its minutes. It has met eleven times to settle a question three ministries disagree on, and its interim finding is that "further study is warranted". No ministry wants to be the one that gave way, and Rajesh Nair, a correspondent who covers the public service for the Straits Times, has started asking you why.

**A. Write a paper that forces a decision.**

> Your paper, with your name in the footer, sets out three options and recommends one. Two ministries object to being cornered, and say so to your superiors. A decision is made anyway, mostly to stop the paper circulating.

↳ Can lead to: [65. The Decision Paper](#c65)

**B. Keep taking excellent minutes.**

> Your minutes are widely admired. The committee meets fourteen more times. At the end you're commended "for your support of the committee's work", and the question is still open.

**C. Tell Rajesh Nair about the deadlock.**

> Rajesh Nair's story runs that weekend, and a decision follows within a month. You were right about what needed to happen. The service remembers, for much longer, that you made it happen through the press.

↳ Can lead to: [66. A Journalist Has Been Asking](#c66) · [Everything Surfaced](#end-scandal)

[← 63](#c63) · [Next → 65](#c65) · [Contents](#contents)

### <a id="c65"></a>65. The Decision Paper

[← 64](#c64) · [Next → 66](#c66) · [Contents](#contents)

When you were secretariat to a deadlocked Inter-Ministry Committee, you wrote a paper that forced it to decide. That committee has now been dissolved, and its replacement has adopted your paper as its terms of reference. The footer still says "Prepared by Ms Lim Wei Ling, Secretariat", and the paper is about to go to every ministry.

**A. Leave your name on it.**

> The footer travels further than you ever have. A Deputy Secretary you've never met asks for you by name.

**B. Take it off before it circulates.**

> It circulates without a name, and in this building that's how everyone finds out who wrote it.

[← 64](#c64) · [Next → 66](#c66) · [Contents](#contents)

### <a id="c66"></a>66. A Journalist Has Been Asking

[← 65](#c65) · [Next → 67](#c67) · [Contents](#contents)

Something confidential got out once, years ago, and you were the one who let it out. Now Rajesh Nair, the Straits Times journalist, is piecing the old story back together, calling everyone who was on the circulation list at the time. Today Mr Nair called you.

**A. Decline to comment. Say nothing to anyone.**

> The story runs without you in it, this time. You spend a fortnight finding out how much of your calm depended on nobody ever asking.

↳ Can lead to: [Everything Surfaced](#end-scandal)

**B. Tell the most senior person you answer to, before the reporter does.**

> It's the worst forty minutes of your career, and it ends the matter for good. You're thanked for coming forward, and it's meant. It's also never forgotten.

<details><summary>This card varies (1 part)</summary>

Each time the card is shown, it is put together from one option of each part. The text above is one of them. Under an option, the words it brings to the rest of the card.

**Part 1**

- Years ago, as the secretariat of an Inter-Ministry Committee, you told Rajesh Nair about its deadlock, which you weren't supposed to discuss. Now Mr Nair rings, without naming anyone, to warn you that a reporter at another paper is rebuilding that old story from everyone who was on the circulation list. The reporter has your number.
- Something confidential got out once, years ago, and you were the one who let it out. Now Rajesh Nair is piecing the old story back together, calling everyone who was on the circulation list at the time. Today Mr Nair called you. *(shown above)*
- Something confidential got out once, years ago, and you were the one who let it out. Now a reporter is piecing the old story back together from everyone who was on the circulation list at the time, and has just called you.

</details>

[← 65](#c65) · [Next → 67](#c67) · [Contents](#contents)

### <a id="c67"></a>67. The Officer You Are Supervising

[← 66](#c66) · [Next → 68](#c68) · [Contents](#contents)

You're supervising a scholar, one of the high-flyers the government paid to study overseas: twenty-six, very clever, and likely to outrank you within four years. The scholar has asked you to look over a first big paper before it goes upstairs. It isn't good. Whatever you say now will be remembered when the scholar is the one above you.

**A. Say so plainly, and teach the scholar why.**

> You say it isn't good, and spend an afternoon showing why. The scholar takes it well, rewrites it twice, and gets rather good. The scholar will outrank you soon, and will remember that afternoon.

↳ Can lead to: [68. The Officer You Taught](#c68) (or, at another grade, [#69](#c69), [#70](#c70), [#71](#c71))

**B. Clear it. Let the scholar find out the hard way.**

> The paper goes up, and goes badly. Three months later the scholar is moved to a better posting anyway, which teaches a different lesson from the one you meant.

**C. Rewrite it yourself overnight.**

> By morning it's a good paper with the scholar's name on it, and you're thanked for it. The scholar never learns to write, and it turns out never needs to.

[← 66](#c66) · [Next → 68](#c68) · [Contents](#contents)

### <a id="c68"></a>68. The Officer You Taught

[← 67](#c67) · [Next → 69](#c69) · [Contents](#contents)

The young scholar you once supervised is your Deputy Secretary now. Back then you said plainly that the scholar's first big paper wasn't good, and spent an afternoon showing why. After a routine meeting your Deputy Secretary stays behind and says, "You were the only person who ever told me my work was bad."

**A. Say it was worth the trouble.**

> Your Deputy Secretary has put your name forward for something, you learn, on the strength of one afternoon years ago. You go back to your desk and sit there for a while.

**B. Say the paper was still bad.**

> Your Deputy Secretary laughs, for the first time in your presence, and agrees. It is, apparently, the only feedback ever worth keeping.

[← 67](#c67) · [Next → 69](#c69) · [Contents](#contents)

### <a id="c69"></a>69. The Officer You Taught

[← 68](#c68) · [Next → 70](#c70) · [Contents](#contents)

The young scholar you once supervised is a Deputy Secretary now, like you, at an age you won't ask about. Back then you said plainly that the scholar's first big paper wasn't good. After a joint meeting the scholar stays behind and says, "You were the only person who ever told me my work was bad."

**A. Say it was worth the trouble.**

> The scholar has put your name forward for something, you learn, on the strength of one afternoon years ago. You go back to your office and sit there for a while.

**B. Say the paper was still bad.**

> The scholar laughs, for the first time in your presence, and agrees. It is, apparently, the only feedback ever worth keeping.

[← 68](#c68) · [Next → 70](#c70) · [Contents](#contents)

### <a id="c70"></a>70. The Officer You Taught

[← 69](#c69) · [Next → 71](#c71) · [Contents](#contents)

The young scholar you once supervised is now one of your Deputy Secretaries. Back then you said plainly that the scholar's first big paper wasn't good. After a routine meeting your Deputy Secretary stays behind and says, "You were the only person who ever told me my work was bad."

**A. Say it was worth the trouble.**

> Your Deputy Secretary now tells new officers the same things, in the same words, and reports that they dislike it just as much. It's said as though this were the point. It was.

**B. Say the paper was still bad.**

> Your Deputy Secretary laughs, for the first time in your presence, and agrees. It is, apparently, the only feedback ever worth keeping.

[← 69](#c69) · [Next → 71](#c71) · [Contents](#contents)

### <a id="c71"></a>71. The Officer You Taught

[← 70](#c70) · [Next → 72](#c72) · [Contents](#contents)

The young scholar you once supervised is a Permanent Secretary now. Back then you said plainly that the scholar's first big paper wasn't good, and spent an afternoon showing why. After briefing a group of MPs on a bill, your old scholar waits until the room has emptied and says, without preamble, "You were the only person who ever told me my work was bad."

**A. Say it was worth the trouble.**

> The favour is returned: from now on, the scholar you once taught tells you when your speeches are bad. It happens often. They get better, and the newspapers notice before the party does.

**B. Say the paper was still bad.**

> A laugh, and the reply that this is why your briefs are now written by a Permanent Secretary personally. They are very good. They are also eleven pages long, and you are expected to read all of them.

[← 70](#c70) · [Next → 72](#c72) · [Contents](#contents)

### <a id="c72"></a>72. The Merlion Speaks

[← 71](#c71) · [Next → 73](#c73) · [Contents](#contents)

At 7.14am the Merlion, the half-lion, half-fish statue on Marina Bay, says good morning to a tour group, in all four official languages. By ten, nobody is asking how. The question is which agency is in charge of it: STB, the Tourism Board, which protects its image by law, or PUB, the water agency, since it spouts into Marina Reservoir. A meeting of both will decide, and you are writing the paper that recommends one.

**A. Recommend STB as lead agency, with PUB supporting.**

> The meeting agrees in one sitting, a record. STB's first decision as lead agency: the Merlion may not speak again until its talking points are cleared. You're put on the working group that will clear them.

↳ Can lead to: [73. Cleared to Speak](#c73)

**B. Ask the Merlion which agency it would prefer.**

> It says PUB, because PUB has never once switched it off for maintenance. Its view is minuted as stakeholder feedback. STB is made lead agency anyway.

**C. Propose a joint working group, chaired in rotation.**

> The working group, with you on it, meets every month for three years at the waterfront, so that the Merlion can attend. It never misses a meeting and, as agreed, says nothing until its talking points are cleared.

↳ Can lead to: [73. Cleared to Speak](#c73)

[← 71](#c71) · [Next → 73](#c73) · [Contents](#contents)

### <a id="c73"></a>73. Cleared to Speak

[← 72](#c72) · [Next → 74](#c74) · [Contents](#contents)

Years ago, when the Merlion first spoke, you sat on the working group set up to clear what it could say. Its talking points have finally been approved: one page, signed off at four levels, the last of them this morning. On Saturday the Merlion will read them to a tour group, and you are invited.

**A. Go, and stand at the back.**

> It reads the page word for word. Then it sees you and says good morning, which was not on the page. Nobody at STB can say who cleared that.

**B. Send your apologies. Ask for the transcript.**

> The transcript matches the talking points exactly, except for one closing line, annotated in red: "Not cleared. Well received."

[← 72](#c72) · [Next → 74](#c74) · [Contents](#contents)

### <a id="c74"></a>74. Pick Three

[← 73](#c73) · [Next → 75](#c75) · [Contents](#contents)

Your division has the money for a one-year pilot of nurses visiting seniors at home in the week after discharge, in three towns, and you choose which three. If it works, the Minister will announce it in the Committee of Supply debate, Parliament's yearly debate on each ministry's budget, in two years, using the numbers your pilot produces. Where you run it will decide what those numbers look like.

**A. Pick the three likeliest to make it work.**

> You pick three towns with busy volunteers and short queues. The launch has a ribbon, a banner and a photographer, and the photograph goes upstairs captioned "Good start."

↳ Can lead to: [75. One Number](#c75)

**B. Pick the three hardest, to learn what breaks it.**

> You pick the three towns where schemes go to struggle. The launch is quiet. Within a month you know more about why things fail than anyone else in the building.

↳ Can lead to: [75. One Number](#c75)

**C. Draw the sites by lottery, and hold half back as a control.**

> A lottery picks three towns to get the pilot and three more to go without, for comparison. The three without get a letter explaining why, and draw more complaints than the pilot does. Your results, though, will be very hard to argue with.

↳ Can lead to: [75. One Number](#c75)

<details><summary>This card varies (1 part)</summary>

Each time the card is shown, it is put together from one option of each part. The text above is one of them. Under an option, the words it brings to the rest of the card.

**Part 1**

- a counter that answers questions about any government payout
  - community clubs
  - the payout counters
- advisers who walk into neighbourhood shops and explain the grants
  - towns
  - the shop visits
- a Sunday counter for salary claims
  - dormitories
  - the Sunday counters
- retired teachers reading with Primary 2 pupils after school

### <a id="c75"></a>75. One Number

[← 74](#c74) · [Next → 76](#c76) · [Contents](#contents)

The pilot you designed, a counter that answers questions about any government payout, has finished, and its evaluation is in. The Minister's Committee of Supply speech needs one number from it by Friday, and whatever you send is the number the country will hear. In the three community clubs you picked, take-up was 71%. But page 40 of the evaluation points out that those three would have taken up almost anything.

**A. Send the most impressive number, on its own.**

> 71% goes into the speech on its own, and the Minister reads it with feeling. The warning stays where it was: on page 40.

↳ Can lead to: [76. Going National](#c76) (or, at another grade, [#77](#c77), [#78](#c78))

**B. Send the number with the paragraph that explains it.**

> 71% goes up with its paragraph from page 40, and the speech keeps both: "in places that were already well served". No other line in the speech gets less applause.

↳ Can lead to: [76. Going National](#c76) (or, at another grade, [#77](#c77), [#78](#c78))

**C. Ask for one more year before anything is announced.**

> The speech goes ahead without your pilot. You spend another year running it in ordinary community clubs, not ones picked to like it. The number you get there is smaller, and you trust it.

↳ Can lead to: [76. Going National](#c76) (or, at another grade, [#77](#c77), [#78](#c78))

  - 71% goes into the speech on its own, and the Minister reads it with feeling. The warning stays where it was: on page 40.
  - 71% goes up with its paragraph from page 40, and the speech keeps both: "in places that were already well served". No other line in the speech gets less applause.
  - The speech goes ahead without your pilot. You spend another year running it in ordinary community clubs, not ones picked to like it. The number you get there is smaller, and you trust it.
- In the three hard community clubs you picked, take-up was 23%. For those three, that's remarkable. In a speech, without the context, it's just 23%.
  - You send the figure from the best of the three: 64%. It's true of that one. The speech doesn't say which one, and nobody asks.
  - 23% goes up with its context, and the speech uses both. The line gets no applause, and the Minister's office asks, kindly, whether there was a better number.
  - The speech goes ahead without your pilot. A second year in the same three brings take-up to 31%, which nobody upstairs asks about, and which you believe.
- Compared with the three community clubs that went without, the pilot raised take-up by four percentage points: a real gain, properly measured, and much smaller than anyone had hoped.
  - You send the gain in the pilot's best month: nineteen points. Every word is accurate. The speech says "up to nineteen points", and the Minister says it twice.
  - Four points go up with the paragraph explaining why four is good. The speech uses it. A newspaper calls the result "modest", and quotes your paragraph correctly.
  - The speech goes ahead without your pilot. A second year confirms the four points, and finds that they last. Nobody upstairs asks, and you write it up anyway.

### <a id="c78"></a>78. Going National

[← 77](#c77) · [Next → 79](#c79) · [Contents](#contents)

The pilot you designed as a civil servant, the home visits, is going national: the Minister announces it in the Committee of Supply debate. Nobody in the chamber knows it was yours. Your own speech on the same ministry's budget is due at four, and you could mention it.

**A. Say, in your speech, that you designed it.**

> The chamber is briefly interested. Your old ministry's officials, in the gallery, write something down. In the wrap-up, the Minister thanks you for your "long association" with the scheme.

↳ Can lead to: [79. The Paragraph](#c79) (or, at another grade, [#80](#c80), [#81](#c81), [#82](#c82))

**B. Say nothing about it. It is the ministry's now.**

> You stick to your speech. In the gallery, an official who sat in your pilot meetings recognises you and nods. That's all the credit there is, and it's plenty.

↳ Can lead to: [79. The Paragraph](#c79) (or, at another grade, [#80](#c80), [#81](#c81), [#82](#c82))

**C. Use your speech to ask about the scheme's weak spot, which you know best.**

> You ask, politely, what happens in the towns where it was always going to struggle. The Minister promises to look into it, and does. Your old ministry spends a month answering your question.

↳ Can lead to: [79. The Paragraph](#c79) (or, at another grade, [#80](#c80), [#81](#c81), [#82](#c82))

### <a id="c79"></a>79. The Paragraph

[← 78](#c78) · [Next → 80](#c80) · [Contents](#contents)

Years ago, your pilot of the home visits gave the Minister's speech its best number. The Auditor-General's report now has a paragraph on the national scheme: its budget assumed nineteen points, from the pilot's best month, and it delivered four points. The ministry paid for the difference. The paragraph names nobody yet. The team that ran it is drafting the ministry's reply.

**A. Tell the auditors the number was yours.**

> You write to the auditors that the number was yours. The team that ran the rollout sends you a card, unsigned, which says only "Thanks."

**B. Let the reply rest on the evaluation's caveats.**

> The reply cites the caveats and the word "indicative". The auditors accept it, and nobody asks, out loud, who chose the number.

- The team that ran it is drafting the ministry's reply. *(shown above)*

  - Let the reply rest on the evaluation's caveats.

> Eleven officers get the paragraph with their names above it. Two print it. One, years later, puts it on the first slide of a talk about evaluation, and credits you second.

**B. Send it upstairs, with a short note of your own.**

> Your note is three lines long and never uses the word "I". It does not need to. The paragraph comes up in the next ranking, and so do you.

### <a id="c81"></a>81. The Paragraph

[← 80](#c80) · [Next → 82](#c82) · [Contents](#contents)

Years ago, as a civil servant, you sent a flattering number from your pilot into a Minister's speech. The Auditor-General's report now traces an expensive shortfall in the national scheme to that number. Rajesh Nair, the Straits Times journalist, has found your name in the file, and wants a comment by six.

**A. Say the number was yours, and so was the choice.**

> The piece runs with your quote in the fourth paragraph. Your old ministry sends a short, slightly surprised thank-you. Someone in the party reads the piece twice, and writes something down.

**B. Say the ministry will respond in due course.**

> The ministry does respond, eventually. The piece runs without your quote but with your name: the author of the number, who "did not respond to queries".

[← 80](#c80) · [Next → 82](#c82) · [Contents](#contents)

### <a id="c82"></a>82. The Paragraph

[← 81](#c81) · [Next → 83](#c83) · [Contents](#contents)

Years ago, as a civil servant, you ran the pilot of a scheme that has since gone national. The Auditor-General's report praises the scheme for forecasting its own take-up almost exactly, from your pilot's evaluation. Rajesh Nair, the Straits Times journalist, has found your name in the file, and is writing it up for Sunday.

**A. Give a quote, and name the team that ran it.**

> The piece runs on Sunday with eleven officers' names in it, which the ministry's communications team would not have allowed, and cannot now undo.

**B. Give a quote about what the evaluation taught you.**

> The piece runs on Sunday as a profile, with a photograph of you at a coffee shop that has nothing to do with the scheme. The officers who ran it read it on their phones.

[← 81](#c81) · [Next → 83](#c83) · [Contents](#contents)

### <a id="c83"></a>83. A Year Away

[← 82](#c82) · [Next → 84](#c84) · [Contents](#contents)

The Public Service Division offers to sponsor a master's degree for you: a year at Harvard's Kennedy School of Government, with fees and an allowance paid. It comes with a bond of its own: two more years in the service, served after your scholarship bond ends. Leaving before then would mean paying back the cost, with damages.

**A. Go. Sign for the extra two years.**

> Twelve months, officials from forty countries, and a seminar in which you are asked to explain Singapore. You come back with a master's, two more years on your bond, and someone else in your old job.

↳ Can lead to: [84. The Bank](#c84)

**B. Decline. Your division needs you this year.**

> Upstairs is grateful, in a way that lasts exactly one year. The offer is not repeated. The division, it turns out, did need you, though not as much as it said.

↳ Can lead to: [84. The Bank](#c84)

**C. Do a master's part-time, at night, on your own money.**

> Two years of evenings, \$40,000 of your own, and not one year added to your bond. The degree is the same degree. Nobody at work asks about it, which was most of the point.

↳ Can lead to: [84. The Bank](#c84)

### <a id="c84"></a>84. The Bank

[← 83](#c83) · [Next → 85](#c85) · [Contents](#contents)

You're still serving your scholarship bond, so leaving the service now means paying damages to PSC, the Public Service Commission that paid for your degree, for the years you haven't served. A bank wants you by March, and will pay those damages in full, for all the years left, and call it a signing bonus. Your bond deed also carries the signatures of two sureties, usually family, who promised to pay PSC themselves if you broke it.

**A. Let the bank pay. Resign in March.**

> The bank's cheque matches the damages to the dollar. Your resignation is accepted in one line, and the farewell email from upstairs is warmer than you expected, and shorter.

↳ Can lead to: [The Consultancy](#end-consultancy)

**B. Decline. A deed is a deed.**

> You decline in two lines, and keep the bank's letter in a drawer. The offer stands until June, when a younger officer takes it. You hear about that at a wedding.

↳ Can lead to: [85. Your Scholar](#c85) (or, at another grade, [#86](#c86))

Each time the card is shown, it is put together from one option of each part. The text above is one of them. Under an option, the words it brings to the rest of the card.

**Part 1**

- the two master's years included
- for all the years left *(shown above)*

</details>

[← 83](#c83) · [Next → 85](#c85) · [Contents](#contents)

### <a id="c85"></a>85. Your Scholar

[← 84](#c84) · [Next → 86](#c86) · [Contents](#contents)

Years ago a bank offered to pay off your scholarship bond, and you said no. Since then PSC has made you a mentor to one of its scholars, now three years into a six-year bond. Your scholar asks to meet: a bank has offered to pay off that bond too, and wants an answer by Friday. Your scholar wants to know what you did.

**A. Tell the truth about what you did, and stop there.**

> You tell it plainly: the bank, the letter, the master's you paid for at night so the bond would not grow. Your scholar writes that down, and underlines it.

**B. Tell your scholar to stay.**

> You make the case for staying, with your night classes as proof it can be done. Your scholar stays, and enrols in the same programme, at the same hour.

**C. Tell your scholar to take it.**

> You say what nobody at PSC says: it is a contract, and the bank is paying. Your scholar resigns in March. PSC thanks you for your time as a mentor, in writing.

<details><summary>This card varies (1 part)</summary>

Each time the card is shown, it is put together from one option of each part. The text above is one of them. Under an option, the words it brings to the rest of the card.

**Part 1**

- *Read with this option:*

  **Your Scholar**

  Years ago a bank offered to pay off your scholarship bond, and you said no. Since then PSC has made you a mentor to one of its scholars, now three years into a six-year bond. Your scholar asks to meet: a bank has offered to pay off that bond too, and wants an answer by Friday. Your scholar wants to know what you did.

  **A. Tell the truth about what you did, and stop there.**

  > You tell it plainly: the bank, the letter, the two years added for a master's, eight years of bond in all. Your scholar listens, thanks you, and does not say which way that pointed.

  **B. Tell your scholar to stay.**

  > You make the case for staying, with your own master's as Exhibit A. Your scholar stays, and asks, a month later, how one applies for the sponsorship.

  **C. Tell your scholar to take it.**

  > You say what nobody at PSC says: it is a contract, and the bank is paying. Your scholar resigns in March. PSC thanks you for your time as a mentor, in writing.

- *Read with this option:*

  **Your Scholar**

  Years ago a bank offered to pay off your scholarship bond, and you said no. Since then PSC has made you a mentor to one of its scholars, now three years into a six-year bond. Your scholar asks to meet: a bank has offered to pay off that bond too, and wants an answer by Friday. Your scholar wants to know what you did.

  **A. Tell the truth about what you did, and stop there.**

  > You tell it plainly: the bank, the letter, the master's you turned down because your division needed you that year. Your scholar asks whether it did. You say: that year.

  **B. Tell your scholar to stay.**

  > You make the case for staying, and leave out the master's you turned down. Your scholar stays. The bank hires someone else by June, as banks do.

  **C. Tell your scholar to take it.**

  > You say what nobody at PSC says: it is a contract, and the bank is paying. Your scholar resigns in March. PSC thanks you for your time as a mentor, in writing.

- *The text above.*
